ParlaMint-AT_2019-09-25-026-XXVI-NRSITZ-00089_d8e4179	I should like to welcome the Vice-President of the Republic to the House and give him the floor immediately. – Mr Vice-Chancellor, you have the floor.
ParlaMint-AT_2019-09-25-026-XXVI-NRSITZ-00089_d8e4201	Mr President. High house! For a good decade, we have had to observe an increase in the interest rate of housing, which is clearly above the increase in other cost of living and the relevant indices. There are several reasons for this. The main factors, however, include the partly exorbitant increase in the basic price level following the financial crisis, which affects the rental interest, the new building that remains behind demand, and the strong increase, especially in the cities. One of the causes is also in Austrian rental law. Although our rental right knows legal limitations of the maximum permissible rental rate, these restrictions only apply to a certain part of the housing market. Only in the full scope of the rental law its rules on rental interest are applied. For those apartments which are subject only to the sub-area or even only to the ABGB right of existence, no statutory limit on interest rates applies, and the central regulatory regulation of the interest rate limitation, namely the indicative value system, applies only to buildings and apartments built before 1945. Thus, in much of the housing market, the rental rate is based on the economic interaction of supply and demand, and this naturally leads to a price increase in a structurally scarce property such as the housing and the resulting demand overhang. We see this rise in rent rates not only in Vienna, but throughout the federal territory, and Innsbruck is only the most prominent example of this. The very high rent rate is thus an almost comprehensive phenomenon in Austria. This phenomenon prevents young people seeking housing from entering an independent life and is therefore of extremely important social relevance. Such a development cannot be indifferent to politics, it must develop concepts. Of course, these concepts are diametrically separated: While, on the one hand, an extension of today's rental rates rules to the whole housing market is aimed at making these rules even clearer and clearer, it is proposed, on the other hand, to liberalise the right to rent, to remove the rent restrictions in order to create incentives and to introduce empty dwellings on the market. The one side therefore insists on a stronger and further intervention into private autonomy, the other side speaks for a repressive legislative intervention and trusts in the market. There are different world-view and political positions and different interests. In my opinion, it would be too little to rely on the effectiveness of market mechanisms and to draw the attention of those seeking housing today to the fact that the free market will already be aimed at them. Wherein, in principle, an unlimited rental interest rate formation in urban agglomerations, which is left to market forces alone, shows us a view abroad. In the large European metropolises, young people can no longer afford to live in the urban area – this is true of cities such as Munich and Hamburg, Paris or London. These are developments that we should avoid for Austria in the best possible effort. Of course, it is not only by means of anti-terrorism measures that it is necessary to create additional housing, the provision of housing by public landlords and the anti-tangling effect of non-profit building associations. In my opinion, there is a great deal of importance to this as well as to the civil-law interest-rate restrictions. However, with regard to such limitation arrangements, the existing existing fragmentation of rental law should also be overcome. So it seems to me to be a command of the hour to go in the direction of a uniform rental right as possible. This uniform rental right must, of course, also contain clear accents for the reduction of the rental interest rate level, which also have its effect. for such segments of the housing market, which are not sufficiently regulated today – with certain exceptions for freely funded construction and renovation projects. Let me add another thought about the consistency of housing: In the last two decades, due to the corresponding changes in the rental law, the development has resulted that more and more leases are only concluded for a certain period of time. This, in addition, leads to tenants being forced either to frequent housing changes or, in their desire for stay in the previous apartment, to depend on the good will of the landlord. This weakens the legal position of tenants and contradicts their need for an independent decision on how long they want to stay in an apartment and when they want to change them. I therefore believe that in future reform efforts in the area of rental law, consideration should also be given to time-limits and legal measures should be taken to ensure that permanent leases are re-established. In this context, it is also necessary to avoid restricting, mitigating or imposing excessive interest rates on existing admission rights. It is clear to me that these admissions also lead to distortions and privileges, but it is not served by anyone who is equally bad. The design of rental law is, of course, a fundamental political question. However, in the case of a future rental law reform, the essential needs and legitimate concerns of applicants and tenants must not be lost in sight. - Thank you.

ParlaMint-AT_2019-09-25-026-XXVI-NRSITZ-00089_d8e4874	Mr President. Dear Mr. Vice-Chancellor! High house! Appreciate viewers! [[Intrusion of the Abg. ParlaMint-AT_2019-09-25-026-XXVI-NRSITZ-00089_d8e6205	Mr President. Ladies and gentlemen, ladies and gentlemen! Ladies and gentlemen! I am, of course, in front of you today as an interior minister, but also as a representative of the Foreign Minister, and I thank you for being able to speak on this very important subject now. It is an important issue because it is important for the integrity of the European Union and is a central pillar for the future of the European Community. We believe that, in order to maintain public order and security in the European Union, it is necessary to ensure effective external border protection, but also an orderly asylum system and an orderly migration policy. It is clear that we can only solve the challenges of a globalised migration policy in Europe together, and we have already agreed on a common procedure in Europe, namely in June 2018. At the time, it has been agreed that we need, in particular, effective control of the EU's external borders, external measures such as third countries' cooperation, and internal measures in the European Union that ensure a common asylum and migration policy. Only with this integrated approach can we ensure a common migration policy in Europe and get under control the migration flows we are talking about. How far are we with the implementation of these measures in Europe? – First of all, it should be stressed that Frontex’s reform is progressing, since Austria has made a decisive contribution to the successful conclusion in April 2019. The new Regulation, which was adopted and to enter into force at the end of 2019, aims to ensure that Frontex will be able to support Member States even better in the future, in particular in the area of border protection, in all stages of return and in third-country cooperation. The central element is that there should be a permanent reserve of 10,000 workers, i.e. persons. Third countries that are not directly adjacent to the European Union can now support Frontex in border protection following this reform. In this respect, it is necessary to conclude additional agreements. This is in planning and hopefully also in implementation as soon as possible. However, this has not yet done everything, there are other measures to strengthen EU external border protection in implementation. For example, the legal basis for a new entry and exit system for third-country nationals has been established and a European travel information and travel authorisation system has been implemented. These measures aim to ensure improved control of the entry and exit of third-country nationals into the European Union and from the European Union. In addition, EU databases on migration, border control and crime control are linked in order to ensure the best possible interoperability. This is the intention of closing gaps in information and ultimately ensuring that data available can be linked in a legally compliant way, so that the systems are ultimately made more efficient and thus also the use of resources. In addition to effective external border protection, it is also necessary to reform the common European asylum and migration system. Negotiations for the future common asylum system have been under way for some time and have been under way, as all of us have experienced. The Member States are particularly disagreeable when it comes to responsibility for asylum procedures, and that is the crucial issue. From the Austrian perspective, the following key points should be highlighted: We need a system that is carried by all Member States. We need rules that are accepted and respected by all Member States. In this direction, we must hold joint talks in the European Union and hopefully conclude them successfully. In my view, important elements of an effective European asylum and migration policy are also the reduction of so-called pull factors, the prevention of secondary migration and a consistent return policy. On the other hand, the proposals currently under discussion in the Union on the automatic distribution of asylum seekers pose risks. In this sense, an automatic distribution mechanism does not contribute to a sustainable solution to which I will make a very special effort. In fact, additional pull factors would be created and the business models of the tractors, most of which are highly criminal, would be promoted. Not all of them from distress Gerettets are in need of protection within the meaning of the Geneva Convention on Refugees – of course, in need of protection in the sense of saving life. Each individual case must be checked precisely and already at the external border. This requires efficient procedure management, clear rights and obligations for asylum seekers and effective consequences in the area of tow and asylum abuse. Austria should therefore vote in favour of a mandatory border procedure. The border procedure should take place at the external borders. As such, asylum seekers should stay at the EU's external border until their asylum status – quickly! – was decided. Of course, countries at the external borders need the full support of the European Union in order to be able to carry out this task. Austria is ready to provide this support with know-how and experts, and has already proved it in the past. The third country cooperation is of course also an important part of this overall measure. Migration and border protection should therefore be an essential part of the cooperation of the European Union and its Member States with partners outside Europe. We must succeed in combating economic and political causes of migration in a sustainable way. What is the situation on the migration routes? – That’s where I speak primarily as Minister of the Interior: On the western Mediterranean route, the situation is largely stable. On the so-called central Mediterranean route, there has been a continuous increase in the number of arrivals since May 2019, but at a low level. On the eastern Mediterranean route, however, the situation is completely different. The volatile situation in Turkey and instability in Iran, Syria and Afghanistan lead to increased migration pressure in Greece. There are rising numbers of arrivals. The situation on the route between the eastern Mediterranean and the Western Balkans is therefore quite tense. It is estimated that some 100 000 asylum seekers or migrants in Greece currently reside. There were some 20,000 seizures in Bosnia alone this year. Austria has therefore been even more involved in the Western Balkans for months, because the situation on the Western Balkans is, of course, particularly noteworthy and important for us. Due to the key role of the Western Balkans, Austria is actively committed to supporting these countries. The aim is to launch the EU security standards and to strengthen the capacity in the areas of migration management, return and border control. It must be a common concern of all Member States of the European Union to have on-the-spot control of migration flows. The aim must be to reduce the pressure of migration in this region on a sustainable basis in order to prevent future crises. Accordingly, an intensive exchange with our partners in the Western Balkans takes place. In my capacity as Minister of the Interior, I have already visited the ambassadors from Bosnia-Herzegovina, Serbia and Northern Macedonia, and I have also had an intensive personal discussion with the Slovenian Minister of the Interior in Slovenia. At present, a high-ranking meeting of Greek representatives is taking place in Vienna, in my house, in which this question is also discussed in detail. [[Abg. Kickl: Ui, with the Greeks! Ui!]] My Dear Ones Ladies and gentlemen! Finally, I would like to focus on an important issue from my point of view for co-existence in Europe: This is the issue of the validity of the Schengen area, our free movement and freedom of travel in Europe. It is precisely there that our rules, which we have given ourselves to in Europe, do not work at present. Member States no longer trust each other and therefore create new borders through border controls. It's just showing up like this: Only if we manage to find common rules in the European Union, which, as I said earlier, are accepted by all Member States and which can thus be a basis for the trust of all Member States, will we be able to travel from one Member State to another without border controls. We therefore need rules that work, we need rules that all accept. In this direction, we must work in Europe from my point of view, and I wish that we not only start this work immediately, but also end it, and that is successful. Thank you.

I stand here for a consistent line on the issue of migration. Not for nothing was the motto of the Austrian Presidency “A Europe that protects”, which is more up-to-date than ever. With the new Commission, we have the opportunity to re-start migration policy. The mission letter from the President of the Commission to the Commissioner for the Interior sets out very clear objectives: to equip external border protection with a strengthened mandate from Frontex, to speed up the increase of frontex officials to 10,000, to force the fight against the tractors, and finally to close the gaps between asylum and repatriation and more. The President of the Commission is to be supported, because we must finally get away from the debate on the forced distribution of asylum seekers. It doesn't make sense, nor does it bring us on. [[Congratulations on the ÖVP.]] [[Abg. Kickl: Does the Seehofer know?]] - Yes. The summit in Malta on Monday, however, apparently had the very goal of looking for a distribution key, and this, ladies and gentlemen, is the wrong way. This does not solve the problem, it does not fight the causes of escape, but – quite the contrary – it leads people into a dilemma. In this way, we are once again promoting tow beings, we are building hopes in those who are in the perceptible search for a better life and open up to crossings that are far too often fatal. We must not, however, be divided into good and bad Member States on this issue either. We need to be aware of the signs we send out, and that means that in this model, the rescue of the sea is still the ticket to Europe. In most cases, these asylum procedures are negative and only lead to a return to the countries of origin. Rather, it needs a system based on the principle of mandatory solidarity and common responsibility. Without exception, each Member State must make a contribution, which may consist of humanitarian assistance, the posting of frontex officials or financial support. In any case, Austria has made a very large contribution with the reception of asylum seekers. Although the figures are declining, the challenge still lies in the handling of many procedures, in the often difficult returns and also in the integration of those who have an asylum grounds and, of course, get protection from us. [[Intrusion of the Abg. Kucharovich.]] Of course, we need to support the Member States directly on the Mediterranean, and of course it is our human duty to save people from drowning in the Mediterranean and to ensure proper initial supply. But we need to do much more than that. We finally have to stop people from getting on such wacky boats at all. We can only fight against illegal migration and thus also reduce the death in the Mediterranean by not assuming that Gerettete is the direct way to Europe. This also eliminates the incentives for the tractors. [[Congratulations on the ÖVP.]] A positive example is Egypt. Since 2016, the Egyptian Coast Guard has been saving people out of the sea and returning them to Egypt. That's why almost no one is getting on the way from there. The example shows how important and how good cooperation with third countries can work, and this needs to be expanded. However, the creation of economic prospects in Africa is also important, and that is exactly what the EU-Africa Forum set out to do in December 2018. But when it comes to people who actually have a reason for asylum, the answer can only be resettlement programmes, so that the aid and support can come to those whom they really need. At the time of their arrival in Europe, these people also know that they can actually stay, which is a completely different basis for integration measures. Sebastian Kurz has already launched the trend turnaround at European level in 2018. It is now important to support the future President of the Commission and also the Commissioner for the Interior. My Dear Ones Ladies and gentlemen, we finally need a sustainable European solution to the question of migration. Without a doubt, the answer can only be a common responsibility and can only be carried out through joint efforts. For this I will work with my colleagues in the European Parliament, and this will be an essential focus of the new, coming legislative period. It becomes challenging, but the thing is soluble. Thank you.

ParlaMint-AT_2019-09-25-026-XXVI-NRSITZ-00089_d8e13558	Madam President. Ladies and gentlemen! Ladies and gentlemen! The findings of the BAT study committee have clearly shown that the BAT and its activities need control. The BAT has some of the most fundamental law-intensive and sharpest instruments in the Republic that the rule of law has to offer. These instruments must not, never and never again, act in the sense of a political couleur, so we request that the legal protection officer be established in Parliament. At present, the law enforcement officers are located in the Ministry, which means that this can also decide on the financial and human resources. The BAT affair in particular has shown how difficult it is to maintain independence. The legal protection officers of the Federal Ministry of the Interior, the Federal Ministry of Constitution, the Federal Ministry of State Defense and the Federal Ministry of Finance are to be established in the National Council, such as the People's Procurement and the Court of Auditors; both are distinguished by their independence in their decision-making, in their personnel and financial equipment. An appointment by the National Council makes the legal guardians independent of the government. The National Council shall decide on financial and human resources, and the legal representatives shall be accountable only to the elected parliamentarians. Secret and intelligence services, as they are currently located in the BMI and the Ministry of State Defense, need not only legal but also political control, and for that there are the two subcommittees. They should be given the right to consult directly and to obtain information on written request. [[Congratulations with the SPÖ.]] These rights must be defined as minority rights so that the opposition can carry out parliamentary control as effectively as possible. In any case, the BAT is developing further, in parallel with that, it needs the expansion of parliamentary control. It must be secured.

There were no payment flows to politicians, officials or other decision-makers in the Republic. But it came, and that must already be said, to unwarranted and questionable payment flows within the EADS Eurofighter network. As has been said, our position is consistent with that of the Court of First Instance. Whatever remains is the so-called Darabos comparison; perhaps you remember, that's been a few years ago. At that time, the then SPÖ defense minister in his negotiations significantly deteriorated the position of the Republic of Austria vis-à-vis Eurofighter, with a massive devalue of the existing equipment. The trial judge also notes that the conclusion of this comparison was probably due to the SPÖ election “social fighter instead of Eurofighter”. There was no record of the negotiations, no documentation, and many experts in the Committee have confirmed to us that without this so-called comparison, we now have the more effective aircraft. Ladies and gentlemen! Of course, we have also worked very intensively on the subject of counter-businesses. We say that counter-actions according to this old pattern are no longer acceptable, because we have to take new ways. What we, I think, should take with us for the future is that we have to go new ways there. A new model according to the Swiss model needs to be developed for future counter-actions. This means, first of all, that a maximum of 100 per cent compensation volume – no doubt 200 per cent of the target was too high in the contract at that time –; secondly, if possible, only direct counter-actions related to the production of the equipment to be obtained; thirdly: transparency in terms of objectives, causality and costs, as well as clear Treaty provisions; fourthly: Taking into account small and medium-sized enterprises; fifthly: Focus on innovation and technology transfer – we certainly have a lot of technology and know-how in Austria, and we should use it – further: Taking into account the life-cycle costs in assessing procurement projects, coordinating the activities of future investigation committees with any criminal proceedings. – These are the learnings we should draw from this experience and take with us for the future. I would like to thank you once again for your cooperation. I think we have been able to contribute a lot to the investigation, and in this sense we do not consider another committee of inquiry to be useful in this case if one more request is made. I think Eurofighter III is enough.

ParlaMint-AT_2019-09-25-026-XXVI-NRSITZ-00089_d8e19572	In charge of the affairs relating to women, families and young people belonging to the Federal Chancellery: Madam President. Dear Mr. Vice-Chancellor! Ladies and gentlemen, Ladies and gentlemen! For Austria, the 2014 European prevalence study showed that 5 percent of all women surveyed were affected by physical and sexual violence during the previous 12 months. This means nothing more than that in Austria a woman or a girl is affected by violence every 3 minutes. Women need support. There is a great need for contact points, and I am also very glad that we have these contact points and that there are many dedicated women – some of them I also see at the top of the audience – who are working for these women and girls who are strengthening them, supporting them and giving them the courage and strength to face criminal proceedings against the perpetrator. [[Congratulations with Mr ÖVP, Mr SPÖ and Mr von JETZT.]] I have had many discussions with the intervention agencies, the anti-violence centres, the women's and girls' advisory services. These places know better than we all know here what violence-affected women and girls need to support what offers we need to make them. It is therefore necessary, in my view, always to listen to these institutions, to take their opinions seriously and to include them in our projects for improvements in the protection of victims. Leave You are now addressing some of the points that have been raised in this specific proposal and in the amendments which have now been tabled. I should like to emphasize positively, in the sense of the women and girls concerned, what has already been mentioned by Mr Pfurtscheller, namely the explicit mention of female genital mutilation as a serious injury to the body. I also feel it is positive that, for the first time in this form, a focus will be placed on the advice of hazards. From my point of view, however, the differentiation is already beginning, because I do not understand a risk counseling as a culprit. Risk advice, which is also useful and important, is a short and punctual intervention. A work of perpetrators is longer-term advice and support for the violent person, which is intended to make him change his behaviour in principle in order to prevent sustainable violence. A risk advice is a first positive step, but overall, it needs a comprehensive approach and appropriate resources for the institutions involved in criminal proceedings. Risk and culprit work, as well as those bodies that support the victims, and that they work together. We discussed more budgetary resources for violence in the House last week – it has been mentioned several times – and I am pleased that the corresponding request for more budget resources has been unanimously adopted by you, ladies and gentlemen. At the time, however, I also stressed the need for more budgets at least because the area of violence protection and the women's and girls' advisory institutions has been under-financed for years. From my point of view, however, there are also improvements that are possible now, without using an additional budget. As an example, I would like to take the Bannmeile, which has already been mentioned by Mr Mahrer today, among other things. I should like to quote from the amendment on the grounds that: “In view of the absence of the previous regulation, according to which schools and childcare facilities were explicitly called” – namely the ban on entry and contact – “an extension of the radius of the banana mile is appropriate from fifty to a hundred meters. “ This proposal definitely expresses what other Members have said, namely that the ban on the entry of children's gardens and schools is being removed and the victims are only protected by a ban. I think that the idea of a banana mile is basically positive. This means that the victim can take with him the protection wherever it is. However, if we take out specially protected rooms such as kindergartens and schools from the ban and replace them with the banana mile, we will lose an important protection. I would like to give you an example that the intervention agency has given me and that is so strong that I would like to say to you again: If there is such a massive violent incident in a family that there has also been a ban on entering a kindergarten so far, then the danger or perpetrator could not approach this kindergarten. If he had approached, even if the child was not there, the kindergarten board or the pedagogue could consult the police, and the executive could intervene. This will no longer be possible in this form with the present proposal, because the kindergarten director or kindergarten teacher has to wait until the child approaches 100 metres – which is a certain challenge – then the police could only be called; and until she enters, a violent incident may have occurred. [[Consistency with the SPÖ and the Abg. Holzinger-Vogtenhuber.]] Therefore, my urgent request not to use the really good idea of the Bannmile to replace the well-functioning system of bans. In addition, I would like to mention another example, which is also found in certain parts of this proposal, which is the subject of data transmission. I know we have to take into account data protection, and I consider data protection to be a very high asset, but in terms of victim protection, it is essential, in my view, that the executive, the institutions of victim protection, and the institutions working with hazards and perpetrators can exchange information, discuss with each other in order to be able to really assess a risk and thus make early interventions. Only in this way is effective victim protection possible from my point of view. Therefore, I hope that, of course, in the context of data protection law, the issue of data transmission will be discussed again here. I hope that with these examples – I do not want to overstep your time today – I have been able to show that the strengthening of the protection of victims and the prevention of violence is now possible, but could be further strengthened, and this also without budget increases – I know that the budget has been burdened by the last plenary sessions. Please bear in mind in the forthcoming votes on these requests that, since we started this debate, only 20 women and girls in Austria have been affected by violence. Thank you.

Thank God many work in politics and outside politics to ensure that it does not take another 170 years until it comes to equal treatment in Austria. Since the introduction of the law on equal treatment for women and men in the definition of the remuneration for the private sector in 1979, a great deal has been launched by law – several equal treatment packages, the innovation of the equal treatment law and the introduction of the equal treatment prosecution and the equal treatment commission in 2004. Unfortunately, equal treatment is not yet a matter of course. We have a lot to do with this, for example, the closure of wage and pension schemes. In the last one and a half years, we have brought a lot of things on the way. We have entered into collective agreements for 24 months, we are now included in the minimum pension for five years, and in my opinion – that is important to me – the legal anchoring of the automatic pension division will also be a milestone in equal treatment. ParlaMint-AT_2019-09-25-026-XXVI-NRSITZ-00089_d8e26550	Mrs Bißmann is speaking on behalf of the Group of the European People's Party (Christian Democrats). - Please.
ParlaMint-AT_2019-09-25-026-XXVI-NRSITZ-00089_d8e26571	Mr President. Thank you, Mrs. Minister! Ladies and gentlemen. Thank you to my esteemed colleague Elisabeth Köstinger and her colleagues for this request, which will prohibit the installation of oil boilers in new buildings in the future. This is important, right and really no longer negotiable during the climate crisis. That is why I naturally support this request. When we talk about oil boilers, we talk about heating technology, about the boilers, into which heating oil is poured and burned. Allow me to take a little trip, because I would like to talk about the heating oil! Heating oil is not only chemically identical to diesel, it is also tax-advanced. However, it is much more favourable than diesel. We often talk about diesel privilege, which is urgently abolished, but hardly anyone speaks of heating oil privilege, which is even more urgently abolished and which is far greater than diesel privilege. Diesel is taxed with 40 cents, heating oil with 10 cents. We know the monstrosity of EUR 4.5 billion – these are the Wifo numbers – which flow annually in Austria into the subsidisation of fossil energy sources. In these 4.5 billion euros, however, heating oil privilege is not included, just diesel privilege. Why is this not a tax privilege for heating oil? Only because it is located in another sector? Or because oil is poured into a boiler and not driven into a tank and no vehicle? – Excursion ended. Thus, with the ban on the installation of oil boilers in a new building, we do not save the climate, although that is of course right and good. We also need much higher thermal regeneration rates in the stock of buildings. Studies say by 2040 we must have re-enacted all the houses. This is an annual recovery rate of 5 percent. We are currently rehabilitating less than 1 per cent each year. In the 2030 mission, a 2 percent recovery rate is set. Dear members of the future Environment Committee! Please make sure that the thermal recovery rate in the building stock is increased! It is important to always rehabilitate in combination: Energy exchange plus thermal renovation. If biomass is heated in poorly insulated houses out of soft windows, it is as pointless as heating oil in top-insulated houses to heat. Finally back to the boilers: As I said, the proposal we are debating here is good and right, there should be no installation of oil heating in new buildings in the future. But now we still have a small problem: In the 2014 Energy Efficiency Act, there is a pass that allows traders to have the exchange of old boilers in new oil boilers counted as energy saving measures. This is an indirect promotion of climate-damaging heating oil. After all, the new oil boilers are still in operation for decades and are totally decarbonizing our decarbonisation strategy by 2050. [[Abg. Wöginger: Yes, but with something we will have to heat!]] – We need to promote alternatives. There is still the out-of-the-oil bonus, but this is still an indirect promotion that we need to lift. [[Abg. Wöginger: We don't want anyone to freeze!]] Mrs. Minister, you have recently said in a “profile” interview that you cannot regulate this by regulation. For the explanation only: A queue is the technical term for projects already approved, which cannot be started and implemented immediately due to not available pots, so that some two, three or four years have to wait. This queue has moved in the wind until 2024. We are now able to completely dismantle these by the end of 2020, the same with the small hydropower, even there the queue is completely dismantled. Of course, we have also seen that the successor tariffs for biomass are guaranteed. It is also in the case of biogas, where, in the near future or in the next few years, major tasks await the biogas sector, when it comes to really producing biogas and then feeding into the gas grid and not flowing as it is currently done with mass. In the field of photovoltaics we have a huge boost. This amounts to 36 million euros a year for photovoltaic systems and storage. This is a more than doubling of the previous figure, for the next three years, totaling 108 million euros. So this is a powerful package, where a lot of money is put up, but this, you also have to say, is to be paid by someone. The eco-electric regime is a budget-neutral, self-financing regime, as Mr Duzdar has already mentioned. This is also one thing we always look at, namely, in the sense of a balance: Turn to renewables and pushes of the ecostrom quite, but not in perpetuum, not without borders. We also need to remember that someone has to pay, namely the so-called end-users, households, but also trade and industry, and there is a requirement for responsible policy that costs do not explode. Summa summarum I think, here is a very, very good package, a bridge to the renewable development law, which is being decided today. ParlaMint-AT_2019-09-25-026-XXVI-NRSITZ-00089_d8e29136	Madam President. Thank you, Mrs. Federal Minister! Ladies and gentlemen! Watchers! Dear Colle- gin Schwarz, thank you for your words regarding pharmacies as well as for the recognition and appreciation you have expressed. I am very pleased, of course, as a self-employed pharmacist. I see it the same way! Let us come to this request. As my colleague, Mr Schwarz, has already said, there are two amendments to the law. In short, it is about transferring the authorization to enter into special contracts in the pharmaceutical salary fund from the board members to the supervisors so that people can react faster and more flexiblely when ordering staff. The second point concerns the reduction of the statutory reserves in the pharmacovigilance fund, which should be reduced from a minimum value of two months to a maximum of four months to a value of one month or a maximum of one and a half months, because it has been shown that the deposits in the past were far too high. [[Intrusion of the Abg. Matznetter.]] I am aware that this is a very dry legal matter at this late hour. But it is true that the public pharmacies and also their standing representation, the Austrian Pharmacy Chamber, are regulated by law up to the smallest detail in order not to say, not to say. This is not always for the benefit of the general public, and often it is no longer contemporary. I would like to quote a few examples to illustrate this. For example, in the current version of the Pharmacopoeia Act, it is still established that a public pharmacy may have opened a maximum of 48 hours a week and must hold 2 hours of lunch break. It is still in there that a personal delivery by the pharmacist has to be made at a distance of up to 6 kilometres – but of course only after approval of the Austrian Pharmacy Chamber. It says within it that a Filialapotheke may be a maximum of 4 kilometres away from the headquarters of the public pharmacy, which in reality means that hardly any branches are opened, because you usually do not need a second pharmacy at 4 kilometers distance. In recent years, we have also created very restrictive restrictions on the online trade of public pharmacies in Austria, which restrict Austrian pharmacies in such a way as to limit Austrian pharmacies compared to international competition that the market now belongs virtually exclusively to foreign shipping pharmacies. Many of these points have been prepared by the black and blue government under Minister of Health Beate Hartinger-Klein in a pharmaceutical law novel. This pharmacy law novel is still in the ministry. Mr Zarfl, I hope that you will continue to use the remaining weeks to perhaps set up initiatives in this area, or that it will be up to the next Minister of Health to finally launch these pharmaceutical legislation. We need many more measures in the field of public supply and pharmacies – also to ensure security of supply. I am firmly convinced that we also need an adjustment to the emergency section in the prescription law so that if there is evidence of non-availability of medicinal products the patient does not need to be returned to the doctor, but the pharmacist can immediately maintain the care by providing the patient with an available alternative from home or abroad. This measure was, by the way, actively recommended in the past in a specific case by both the Ministry of Health and the Ages, although the legal framework is not yet in place. The financing of the Ages, the medical market supervision, also belongs to a new regulation. The completely disproportionate contribution of the public pharmacies is reduced and, in accordance with the 'polluter pays' principle, is, for example, converted to a packaging charge in the cent sector. This would also ensure the financing of the Ages in the long term. You see, these are very many legal points, but they are important for security of supply and in which, unfortunately, there has been no agreement between the parliamentary groups in the last few months, which is why we have not been able to successfully bring in the appropriate proposals here. That is why my appeal to the health spokespersons of the political groups is: Dear Gaby Schwarz, dear colleague Kucher – if he is there; no, he is not there – dear Gerald Loacker, I am looking for you all to work hard on these issues so that we can find common applications in the coming weeks and months. [[Abg. Lightness: He's sick!]] We would thus contribute to a significant improvement in the supply of medicinal products to the Austrian population, in addition to adapting the excellent system of public pharmacies to the current requirements. A small first step will be taken here today, together with the present draft law. Thank you.

ParlaMint-AT_2019-09-25-026-XXVI-NRSITZ-00089_d8e31686	(Parliament adopted the legislative resolution) Mrs. Federal Minister! Ladies and gentlemen! High house! I should like to say a few words about Agenda item 21 in the short term. Right to care, say. In 2013, the care care care care scheme was introduced into the law, which requires workers and employers to agree and to conclude an agreement when care needs apply to close relatives in the family. Then, with an agreement in operation, a short time off and a payment of care pay is possible. Now let's go one step further. We know that care is an issue that we have already dealt with in detail during the previous meetings and that we will also have to consider in detail during the next legislative period in order to turn on many different wheels in order to treat the care at home, the stationary care, the financing of care and all the care areas in total. The draft law is that the worker is entitled to a right under certain conditions if he has an urgent need for care in a close family. One requirement is that this right applies only to companies that have more than five employees, because of course it is not quite easy for a company if someone is very suddenly out of work. Nevertheless, our compromise, which we have found, is now to grant this right under these conditions. For the protocol and information a few numbers: In 2017, there were 2,634 care carers in Austria, of which about 22 percent were for the support of severely ill children, 21 percent for a death accompaniment, i.e. the so-called family hospice – the completeness half: There was already a legal claim. And about 53 percent, i.e. approximately 1,500 people, have taken care of care for an agreement with the employer. In total, in Austria we spent 7.8 million euros on state funding for the care allowance. We now decide by a large majority or perhaps even unanimously on this legal claim. This is a small mosaic in the many areas that we need to improve so that care at home and in general gets better. On the part of the People's Party, we have made some proposals for the next legislative period, including the care home bonus we want to introduce. In this sense I hope for broad approval and thank you at the late hour for this new law.

ParlaMint-AT_2019-09-25-026-XXVI-NRSITZ-00089_d8e32457	Madam President. Thank you, Mrs. Federal Minister! Ladies and gentlemen! Dear sisters! We have often discussed plant protection during this legislative period, and unfortunately the debate has often been limited to individual means, to individual active substances. Glyphosate is an example that is still in good memory for all of us. We have not asked ourselves enough the major systemic questions in the field of plant protection. This is not, at least from my point of view, a question of taking out individual active substances or individual means, which may even be replaced by even more harmful means, but rather of how we imagine the question of plant protection in the future. We all know: With climate heating, with less rain or sometimes more rain, it becomes increasingly difficult, and we simply need to understand that we are forcing integrated plant protection, that we are going into research and development, and that we are finding alternatives – alternatives that can also be made to market as quickly as possible and then integrated into the agricultural sector. Why is the subject so important? – It has already been mentioned by my previous speakers: It is about the loss of biodiversity, and it is worryingly high. Since 1986, the number of wild animals in Austria has fallen by 70 percent. Most of this is about birds and insects, and there are two main causes for this: On the one hand, this is the loss of the natural space. This is a very important issue, which we must adopt in the next legislative period. We continue to grow 13 hectares a day – that is something we really need to do, that can't go on! The second reason is the excessive use of chemical-syntthetic plant protection products. [[Abg. Berlakovich: There are several reasons, not just that!]] There we are on that subject that we have already heard today: The amount of chemical-syntthetic plant protection products produced in agriculture does not really change, which is relatively constant in average. Now, like Mr Strasser, we can say that this is good – we believe that there is much more going on, we believe that there can still be significant reductions. We need a turnaround, and this is also the subject of my two requests. We have already heard from the previous speakers that there is great support, I am very pleased. My applications are at the heart of research and development to promote innovation in order to make Austria the technology leader in this field – it is much prepared and we can do well. It is also about the Republic's progress in its participation as a model. I think these are concerns that we can all support well, as we have already heard. I mean, these are concerns that need to be supported across party borders and, I see, get them today. I would therefore like to thank you very much for going along with these two requests and I am looking forward to further cooperation. – Thank you very much.

ParlaMint-AT_2019-09-25-026-XXVI-NRSITZ-00089_d8e32632	Madam President. Dear ladies and gentlemen! I should like to refer briefly to a report on the Court of Auditors on the prevention of corruption. The Court of Auditors has examined four ministries – the Federal Chancellery, the Ministry of the Interior, the Ministry of Education and the Ministry of Agriculture – which compared the anti-corruption measures, the objectives assessed, risk and weakness analyses, the communication and further development of these measures by the Court of Auditors. A total of 46 recommendations have been made, and it has been shown that there are some gaps. In some cases, only partial aspects have been fulfilled, especially as regards training, as there is quite a need for re-examination. None of the four Federal Ministries carried out a range of risk and risk analysis in relation to the risk of corruption. There is also quite a need for improvement in terms of information. Overall, the field of lobbying has also been reviewed. In particular, the reporting of secondary employment was criticised; the assessment of the admissibility of secondary employment was primarily dependent on the staff themselves. In summary, it can be said that the implementation of three measures needs to be called for: a sector-wide risk and risk analysis with regard to the risk of corruption, complementary measures to strengthen integrated behaviour and information in the field of lobbying. So this is a total Court of Auditors report with different assessments, but there is a clear mandate to see this issue as a whole in the future and to eliminate the existing weaknesses. Thank you.
